# Open and deferred items

Resolved items are folded into SPEC.md and removed — this file holds only what's still open. Numbers are from the continuous project-wide sequence; never renumber or reuse.
## Deferred until the sim produces evidence

925. **Three more things git knows about overlapping patches, parked** (raised 2026-08-27 with 924; **Ed: park for now** — each wants design work on how it is communicated before it is worth a mechanism). **(a) Dependent candidates.** `propose-edit` (K24) starts a draft from a candidate's wording, so C can be built on unadopted A; if A loses, C's rebase onto the incumbent fails over a decision made elsewhere. *Presupposes* is a different relation from *composes* (§2.2's lattice), and git's stacked patches say: declare it at submission, adopt in order, and let C fall with A or return to its author with A's fate stated. Verify what the engine does today first. **(b) A candidate's version chain.** §2.4's blanket *evidence resets* does not distinguish a clean rebase (no words changed) from an author's revision; Mercurial's obsolescence markers give a change an identity across rewrites, each recording what it superseded and why, so the rule can be *rebase keeps, revision resets*. With it, git's `rerere`: an author's confirmation against new text is remembered and reused when the same span moves again, instead of the leader failing identically every batch. **(c) Two cheap ones**, no spec change: a base-independent content hash of the normalised hunk (`patch-id`) before the dedup gate asks the LLM, and an interdiff — what moved under you, not the whole new paragraph — on the confirm-against-new-text card.

26. **Evidence-clock adoption threshold.** Wall clock chosen for v1 (Q22, options 23–25, 2026-08-13): simpler, knowable, one clock shared with the token drip. Revisit the evidence-clock (or a hybrid max-of-both) ramp as a sim A/B once everything else works.

## Open

49. **Naming and typing disputes** (Ed's question, 2026-08-14): the engine knows a dispute's *location* (span/footprint), not its *nature*. Labels in UI currently fall back to nearest markdown heading + excerpt, which fails on heading-less documents. P3 should generate both a name ("treasurer oversight") and a type (copy-edit vs substantive vs structural) per race — the type also informs routing (copy-edits shouldn't burn diagonal attention slots) and the record. *Interim landed 2026-08-14 (5e61969): advisory `describeRace` oracle capability + `race-labeler` with deterministic nearest-heading fallback; type stored but deliberately unwired from routing. Still open: wire type into routing (needs a SPEC §8 sentence), full P3 treatment.*

52. **Composer design decisions** (raised 2026-08-14, unanswered; **narrowed 2026-08-16**). The composer was rebuilt into session-view as in-place editing (224–241), and design/composer.html’s drafting desk and arrival bar went with it — so (a) and (c) below now describe a surface that no longer exists, and survive only as questions about the **briefing**, which is still to be rebuilt as an escalation state (SPEC §3.5). (b) is untouched, since the dedup-gate has not been built anywhere yet. Three calls made in design/composer.html that Ed has not ruled on. (a) The standings-panel draws the incumbent's bar as its *certification* — P(current text beats best challenger) — so the display shows why only a bridge has a path; the spec doesn't dictate how the composer renders standings. (b) The dedup-gate borders purple, extending the series' "changed since you looked" grammar to "the engine interrupting your submission" — defensible stretch or grammar dilution. (c) The desk deliberately never predicts how your own draft would poll (a self-poll would be a standings feed by the back door) — spec-faithful, but a participant might expect it.

53. **Candidate states missing from the spec** (found 2026-08-14): SPEC §2.6 lists state {live, adopted, retired, merged, carried, withdrawn}, but engine-core's types.ts carries `displaced` and `rebase-pending`, which the mechanism needs. Fold them into §2.6, or rule them implementation detail and note why.


70. **design/race-card.html is retired** (opened 2026-08-15; settled by Ed 2026-08-16). The overlay model and the comparison modes (marked-up / fair copy / differences) are dropped rather than ported — session-view's lanes do the job, and a toggle between three renderings of the same pair is machinery a surface built for typo-fixes should not carry. The one thing race-card.html held that mattered, the **salience diagonal**, is now built here (Q221). What remains: **answered by Ed 2026-08-28 (from the CLAUDE.md audit's C4) — delete `design/race-card.html`.** Owed, not built, and left here until it is, because the deletion is not a one-liner: `CLAUDE.md`'s `race-card` glossary entry names the path with a `[file]` kind and `spec-check` resolves those, `design/spec-pass/card-audit.html` cites it, and the stale promise in session-view's race footer — *full race view has comparison modes and the whole queue* — goes with it. Nothing further should be built on that file meanwhile.

72. **Queue-wire reach** (found 2026-08-15, from Q71; trigger settled by Q78 — the wire belongs to the open card only). Two calls I made that Ed hasn't ruled on. (a) The wire lands on the anchored **paragraph(s)**; Ed's phrasing was "left into the decision cards", which could instead mean the open inline card. (b) There is no wire in the other direction — hovering a paragraph in the document doesn't light its queue entry — though the relationship is symmetrical and the return trip is arguably the more useful one when you are reading rather than working the queue.

77. **Where the scroll runway lives** (raised 2026-08-15, unanswered): removing the design notes (76) took most of the page's scroll with them, so the document now carries ~95vh of bottom padding — without it an anchor near the end of the charter can never be scrolled up to the queue rail and the wire-levelling (74) silently stops working down there. I put that padding *inside* `.doc`, so the middle column literally runs to the bottom as Ed asked; the cost is that scrolling to the end shows about a screenful of blank paper below the last line. The alternative is padding on `main` instead: the card stops just after "Adopted at the house, by the fourteen, over pasta" and the runway is page background. One line either way.

89. **Where Skip lives, and how its decay is shown** (raised 2026-08-16; Ed: skip should not be on the decision card at all — it will probably live in the `needs-you-queue` once that surface is worked on; left in place for now). Two parts remain. (a) The move itself, which waits on the queue work. (b) The mockup does not model SPEC §8.3's "skipped cards recirculate personally with decay" — skipping closes the card and leaves the queue entry exactly where it was (superseded in part: Q174 proposes it should drop the entry to the cool tier instead), so nothing conveys that it will come back less often. Whether that needs any acknowledgement at all, or whether silence is right, is undecided. Worth settling with (a), since a skip that visibly *moves* something in the queue would answer it for free.

92. **Should a race lane be marked against the incumbent?** (opened 2026-08-16 from Q91 as a question about the change card, which no longer exists — Q203 removed it, so only this half survives.) Quick and patch lanes light their new wording green; a race lane shows no highlighting at all, because both sides are whole alternatives with no baseline to mark against. Marking each candidate against the *current* text is possible and would make all three cards identical in treatment, but on a wholesale rewrite it lights up nearly every word, which is noise rather than information. Left unmarked on that reasoning, but it is the one place the three card kinds still differ.

94. **A rationale is machine-drafted and human-edited** (opened 2026-08-16; settled by Ed 2026-08-16). The composer generates a first draft describing the change in mechanical terms — what it drops, what it adds — and the author edits it into their own words before submitting. This is the option I had been most wary of, and Ed's addition of the edit step is what fixes it: the words are the author's by the time anyone reads them, so nothing machine-written ever appears on a blind card under a human's name. It also recovers what the removed ledger was for, because a mechanical draft names the losses by construction and an author has to actively delete them.

    Consequences to settle before the composer is built. (a) **SPEC §2.6** currently says a candidate carries "one pinned rationale (≤300 chars)" and §3.4 that "to argue is to draft" — neither is wrong, but neither anticipates a drafting aid, and the spec should say whether the draft is a *suggestion* the author may discard wholesale (it should be). Needs Ed's sign-off and a version bump. (b) **Conditional on the LLM API**, per Ed: with no oracle the field is simply empty and the author writes from scratch, so the composer must not depend on it. *Built that way 2026-08-16: the rationale is an empty editable with a placeholder, and nothing in the composer calls an oracle. A drafted first line would drop into it without any other change.* (c) The **event log** should probably record that a rationale started from a draft, since "authored by a human" is a claim the record makes; unresolved whether that is a per-candidate flag or nothing at all. (d) A drafted rationale must never be *submitted* unedited by default — an empty-handed author pressing through would put machine prose on a card, which is the failure mode the edit step exists to prevent.

97. **SPEC §8.3 promises a prompt the card no longer has** (found 2026-08-16, from Q96's resolution). §8.3 reads "cards never offer 'keep the current text' on a rival pair … and the rival card's prompt states the conditional framing plainly." Ed removed those prompts in 95 and ruled the absence fine — members needn't learn the rules on every card. The first half of the sentence is still binding mechanism; the second half is now a false description of the product. Needs a one-clause amendment and a version bump, which wants Ed's sign-off since it is the spec.

174. **Skip in a margin index: what does it actually do to the entry?** (raised 2026-08-16, Ed's question, from Q89.) Ed asked whether deselecting a card should mean skip; answered no — closing is browsing, and binding a signal to the navigation gesture makes the signal dishonest (SPEC §3.1: "Skipping is not a move; the card returns later", and §8.3's only effect is personal recirculation with decay). So skip stays an explicit control, and should move into the rail per Q89. But the rail is a **margin index**, not a conveyor, and that changes what skip can mean: the entry cannot vanish, because the clause still has an open question and the margin is supposed to tell the truth about the document. Proposal to settle: skipping drops the entry to the **cool** tier — one line, palest wash — at its own clause, so it stops asking without stopping being there, and the decay is legible as "this went quiet". A second skip on an already-cool entry could compress it further. Related: SPEC does not specify the decay function at all (Q175), so "comes back less often" has no numbers behind it yet.

175. **The skip decay function is unspecified.** SPEC §8.3 says skipped cards "recirculate personally with decay" and stops there — no half-life, no floor, no interaction with the re-opened-race boost or the unheard boost. Needs a number before the sim can test it, and the answer probably wants sim evidence rather than a guess.

### Queue ordering — what survives Ed's 104–108

Ed replaced ranked ordering with a **margin index** (2026-08-16): entries stand beside their own clauses in document order, sealed items are dots, a patch appears once per site. Urgency was carried by colour strength and card size; since 2026-08-16 the card sizes are equal and urgency instead decides **which** entries fit on the screen, so the number now gates visibility rather than compression — which makes 98 below considerably more load-bearing than it was. That closed 99 (document position doesn't move when others judge), 101 (sealed stay, as dots — the rail is a map, and a map shows settled ground) and 103 (there are no serving *slots* left to explain; everything is shown at once). Three remain, all about the urgency number itself rather than about order.
98. **What is a race's urgency?** SPEC §8.1 scores each *pair* by pivotality v. A race with six candidates has many pairs of differing v; the rail shows the race once. Is that number the v of the single pair it would serve you next (honest — it's what clicking gets you), the max over its servable pairs (its potential), or a sum (its total remaining work, which would leave big races permanently at the top of the rail)? The mockup hand-authors one `urgency` number per entry and dodges the question. Now that urgency decides what you can see rather than how loudly it is drawn, a wrong answer here costs a member sight of a question rather than a shade of colour.

100. **Where c_p goes now.** A participant's judgment cost is one scalar dividing every v, so it never reorders their own list — and with the rail showing everything at once it can't truncate one either. It has to act on the *drawing*: since 2026-08-16 the rail already shows only as many questions as fit, most urgent first, so c_p's natural home is that cut — an expensive judge is shown fewer of them, and the rest stay in the count at the foot of the rail rather than being hidden. Undecided, but the mechanism it would act on now exists.

102. **Urgency is kind-blind.** Nothing distinguishes a copy-edit from a constitutional fight, so a typo near its floor can burn as bright as a structural race. Ed's north star ("most of a session should feel like approving typo fixes") argues for letting cheap items shine; the risk is a margin lit up by typos while a structural race sits pale. The `race-labeler` already stores copy-edit/substantive/structural but nothing consumes it (Q49) — the same decision arriving from the other end.

109. **Scale: the margin assumes a short rail.** Sixteen entries beside a twenty-page charter is a map you can scan. Two hundred would be a filing system, and document order would stop steering anyone toward decision margins at all — which is what §8.1 exists to do. V1 tunes to 5–10 members, so this is not urgent, but it is the assumption the whole design now rests on and it should be named before anyone builds for a bigger room.

111. **An open patch cannot keep its sites level and stay on screen** (found 2026-08-16, building 110). The decision card opens inline at the patch's first site and is tall, so the second and third sites move hundreds of pixels down the document — further than the visible band. Ed's 110 says needs-you entries stay on screen, so the sibling entries hold at the band edge and their wires run at an angle; only the topmost is level. Three ways out: (a) leave it — the spine still cables them and each wire still points at its own site, so the relationship is legible even when the geometry isn't; (b) let a patch's siblings leave the band while it is open, treating an open judgment as an exception to 110; (c) open the decision card somewhere that doesn't displace the other sites — a fixed panel rather than an inline insert, which is a much bigger change and cuts against the whole in-place design. I would take (a), but it is the first place where 108 and 110 genuinely pull against each other.

113. **Where read state lives, and when a decision goes quiet** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q112; part (c) settled by Q114 — an explicit acknowledgement is the only thing that clears an unread decision, so nothing marks itself read). Read/unread is the first per-member state on this surface — everything else in the rail is identical for everyone. Two things remain. (a) It has to be **stored somewhere**: a per-member marker in the event log is the obvious home, but an acknowledgement is not a judgment and probably should not be hash-chained with them; a side table keyed by member and candidate is more honest, at the cost of the log no longer being the whole story. (b) **Does an unread decision ever give up?** Ours pins forever, and now that only an explicit OK clears it, that matters more: a member away for two sessions returns to a rail of purple lines. Ed's read (2026-08-16) is that this is survivable because 115 protects the most urgent live card regardless — whatever else is crowded out, the thing that most wants them is on the screen. So the failure mode is a cluttered rail, not a lost one. Still worth deciding whether some relief is wanted — expire at a session boundary, cap how many pin at once and count the rest, or an *acknowledge all* — since the clutter is what the overflow count in 110 will be reporting.

122. **When do rankings become visible — at seal, or at close?** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q121.) A sealed race now shows its full field ranked, with each candidate's win probability against the current text. The warrant is good: Bradley–Terry already carries a strength per candidate, SPEC §8.3's no-standings rule governs *live* feeds (where knowing who is ahead makes judging strategic), and the `record-builder` publishes rankings at close regardless. But this is still rankings appearing **during** a session rather than after it, which is a small extension nobody has ruled on. Two things to weigh. (a) Sealed races are independent of live ones, so there is no direct leak — but seeing standings on settled questions normalises the idea that standings exist, and a member may start reading the sealed ones for camp structure. (b) It gives an author immediate, precise feedback on how their proposal did, which is arguably the humane thing and arguably a reason to redraft on scoreboard rather than on argument. If Ed wants rankings held until close, the card can show the outcome and the field without the numbers, and the numbers arrive with the record. Needs a SPEC §8.3 sentence either way.

124. **Teasers made the rail say more about fewer things** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q123). Quoting rationales grew a hot card from ~76px to ~117px and a two-teaser race to ~164px, so 110's fit cap now drops four or five of twelve entries on an 800px window instead of none. The overflow count reports them honestly, and this is the first time that rule has fired outside a contrived test — but it is a genuine trade and nobody has chosen a side. Options: (a) accept it — a margin that says something about eight clauses beats a list that names twelve; (b) clamp harder — one line on hot as well as warm, which roughly halves the growth; (c) show the teaser only on the entry nearest your reading position, so at most one or two are expanded at a time and the rest stay compact. (c) is the most interesting and the most fiddly.

125. **The open card duplicates its own teaser.** While a suggestion is open, its rationale appears twice on screen — clamped in the rail entry and in full on the card a few inches away. Harmless but slightly silly. The entry could drop its teaser while open, or the card could drop the rationale on the assumption you have just read it (clearly wrong — the card is the place it belongs). Probably (a), but it interacts with 124(c).

159. **The re-served race after a ground shift isn't modelled** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q156). When a race's ground shifts, the old judgment locks *and* fresh pairs are served — so the clause should carry two rail entries, the stranded 🔄 stub and a new needs-you card. The mockup shows only the stranded half (§ The Garden). Needs a decision: do they stack at the clause, does the stub collapse into the new card's history, or does it go straight to a filed dot?

161. **Does the filed state split by outcome too?** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q160.) An unacknowledged decision now says which way it went — ✅ the charter changed, ❎ the incumbent held. Once acknowledged it becomes a single ☑️ regardless, so the outcome is only visible until you press OK, after which the margin says "something was settled here" but not what. That may be right (a filed decision is a bookmark; the record is one click away) or it may be a loss (a row of dots down a section is exactly where you'd want to see at a glance which clauses moved). If it should split, ☑️ needs a partner — and unlike ✅/❎ there is no matched pair in the emoji set, which is a small argument for switching the filed mark to a reduced-opacity ✅/❎ instead. Interacts with 158(b), since ☑️'s purple is already a problem.

### Marks for the other asks (raised 2026-08-16, Ed's question; awaiting his pick)

The deadlocked case showed that these marks say *what you are being asked to do*, not where in a lifecycle something sits. Settled 2026-08-16 (166–167, 173): a deadlocked race is ❌ *stuck*, in the open yellow rather than grey, and 💡 marks the propose actions it leads to — the rail states, the buttons act. Still open:
168. **Bridge wanted** — a deadlocked race where camps have been detected (SPEC §6.3) asks for something sharper than any new draft: one that spans two positions, which the composer briefs for. Own mark, or 💡 with a different briefing?

169. **Your candidate was dominated** (SPEC §8.4 already names it as a digest item). The ask is revise-or-withdraw, addressed to you as author.

170. **Your candidate needs rebasing** — engine-core carries `rebase-pending` and `displaced` (Q53). Distinct from 🔄, which is your *judgment* stranded; this is your *draft* stranded, and only you can move it.

171. **Does the rail carry authorship at all?** Every state built so far is you-as-judge. 169 and 170 are you-as-author, and so are the composer, the graveyard and the token ledger. Whether that belongs in this rail or on a separate surface is a bigger question than any symbol, and 169–170 wait on it. *Partly answered 2026-08-16 by building the composer: the rail does carry authorship, as the green `yours` state, and it works — a proposal of your own stands beside its clause like everything else and is force-kept on screen because withdrawing is always available (Ed, 240). So the placement question is settled, and 169–170 become questions about **marks** rather than about **place**: a dominated candidate and a rebase-pending one are both things that happen to a green entry, at a clause that already has your entry beside it. What is still open is whether they are their own glyphs inside green, or a line on the card.*

182. **A wide patch is now a walk, not a view** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q181). Splitting the patch card into one card per site pushes its places much further apart than the old stacked lanes did — three cards of ~450px between them — so `foldBetweenSites` (Q80) no longer brings the whole footprint onto one screen, which was its whole purpose. The stepper answers it for three sites. At six or ten it is less clear: the member never sees the shape of what they are approving, only a sequence of local views, and the only thing asserting "these are one change" is the wire spine and the footer text. Options if it bites: a "show all places" summary panel inside the first card (reintroducing the old view as an option rather than the default); a compact map of the footprint in each card's header; or a cap above which a patch reverts to the stacked form. Wants a fixture with a wider patch before deciding — the current one has three sites, which is comfortably inside the range where this works.

185. **Does "2 of 3 places" go too?** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q184.) Ed named three subtitles to remove — *copy edit*, *n proposals racing*, *one suggestion* — all of them category labels, and the kind chip with them. I kept the patch place count, on the reading that it is not a category but a *position*: it says which of a patch's clauses you are beside, which nothing else on the card does, and with the chip gone it is the only thing distinguishing three sibling entries from three unrelated ones. If the instruction was "clear the whole subtitle line", it is a one-line removal — but then a patch's siblings are told apart only by the wire spine when one is open, and by nothing at all when none is.

187. **Should a live card show the floor as a headcount too?** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q186.) The navbar now says *voices needed 5 of 14* and the sealed record says *5 of 14 weighed in · floor was 5*. A **live** card shows only the evidence-meter and its caption, and § Front-door Keys is the one entry whose caption mentions the floor at all ("three of the fourteen have weighed in — the floor is 5"). Making that consistent — every live card stating "n of 14 so far, floor 5" — would be honest, magnitude-only, and probably the single most reassuring number on the surface. The reason to hesitate is that a running judge-count is *nearly* directional: watching it climb tells you the race is being sampled, and combined with the meter it narrows what the posterior can be. Probably safe (SPEC §8.2 puts per-race judge counts in the record and the mockup already exposes one), but it should be a decision rather than a drift.

189. **"Hot", "warm", "cool" are three different vocabularies wearing one word** (raised 2026-08-16, Ed's question — he asked where the terms come from and whether they are well defined; the honest answer is that two of the three are mine and one is the spec's, and they do not mean the same thing). (a) SPEC §8.3's **hot set** is a real defined term: the ~3 races receiving concentrated sampling at any moment — a property of the *router*, global, the same for everybody. (b) SPEC §3.2's **care-map cold** is also defined: a span the group is indifferent about, which keeps its incumbent cheaply and sinks in the feed — a property of the *group's* attitude to a clause. (c) The mockup's **hot / warm / cool tiers** are neither: they are three buckets of the per-member `urgency` scalar at 0.66 and 0.33, invented for the rail's compression ladder (Q104), personal rather than global, and unrelated to (a) or (b) — a card can be mockup-"hot" while not being in the router's hot set, and a care-map-cold span would show as mockup-"cool" only by coincidence. Three fixes, pick one: rename the tiers to something with no thermal meaning (`full` / `brief` / `line`, describing what is drawn rather than what is meant); rename them to what they measure (`yours-most` / `yours` / `background`); or define the collision away by making the tiers actually derive from the router's hot set, which would be a mechanism change rather than a naming one. I lean to the first — the tiers are a *drawing* decision and naming them after the drawing keeps them honestly separate from both spec terms.

196. **Where should the tier thresholds sit?** (raised 2026-08-16, Ed's ask; the fixture is now rich enough to answer it — Q194 done.) With ten more suggestions spread down the urgency range, the current cuts at **0.66 / 0.33** give 3 hot, 8 warm, 8 cool. The middle bucket being the largest is probably the crowding Ed felt. Three ways to rebalance: (a) raise the warm/cool cut to ~0.45, which moves four entries down to one-liners and leaves ~4 warm; (b) raise both, to ~0.75 / 0.45, which also thins hot to 2 and makes the flame rarer still; (c) stop using fixed cuts and take the top *n* by urgency for each tier, so the ladder's shape is stable however many suggestions exist — the honest version if the rail is meant to feel the same at 20 suggestions and at 60. I lean to (c) with n≈2 hot and n≈5 warm, but it should be looked at rather than reasoned about.

207. **Nothing now says which lane is the current text** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q206). With "Option A/B" gone (197) and the lane descriptions gone too, a decision card is two boxes of prose. Which one is the incumbent is carried by **position** — left, always — by the fact that the left lane matches the paragraph directly above the card, and by a footer clause saying that choosing the current text records a judgment for it. That is probably enough for a member who has judged a few, and it is the strongest symmetry the card has ever had. The risk is the first card someone ever sees, where the convention has not been learned yet. Cheapest mitigations if it bites: a one-line prompt above the lanes on a member's first few cards only; or a very quiet marker on the incumbent lane that is not a title. Wants a real reader before deciding.

208. **Skip now exists only on the 🔥 card** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q202 — Ed's call, with the reasoning that skip belongs where the surface *insists* and nowhere it merely offers). Two loose ends. (a) SPEC §3.1 and §8.3 describe skipping as available on any card, so either the spec gains a sentence saying it is a property of *insistent* serving rather than of cards, or the product is knowingly narrower than the mechanism. (b) The personal recirculation-with-decay that skip triggers now applies to at most one suggestion at a time, which may make the decay function (Q175) nearly irrelevant — worth checking in the sim before anyone spends effort calibrating it.

211. **Undoing indifference costs two clicks** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q209). The shrug hides once anything is chosen, so an indifferent verdict can only be cleared by picking a lane and then unpicking it. A lane's own undo is one click. Options: leave it (indifference is rarely a slip); keep the shrug visible-but-pressed alongside Submit, which is what 204 built and 209 replaced; or let Submit's *own* neighbour be a quiet "clear" while something is chosen. Minor, but it is the only place on the card where undo is asymmetric.

212. **Closeness is now only on the queue card** (raised 2026-08-16, from Q210). Removing the `evidence-meter` from decision cards leaves closeness-to-resolution — and a race's "you've judged 2 of the pairs you'll be asked for" — visible *only* in the rail. A member who opens a card by clicking the paragraph in the document, rather than its queue entry, sees neither. Three readings: it does not matter, because the rail entry is a few inches away and lit; it matters for the coverage line specifically, which is personal and has no other home; or the card should carry a single quiet line of both. The coverage half has been in this state since 95 without complaint, which is weak evidence for the first reading.


264. **Green now means three things** (raised 2026-08-16; widened the same day by the fix to 263). In a rail or a gutter it means *yours, and you can still act on it*; inside a card it means *decided* — the winning text in a sealed record, the tick that commits — and inside a **lane** it means *new wording*, which it has meant since 91 and now appears in the composer as well as on every decision card. They never meet, because 164 washes every decided state grey in the margin, so `--ok` is confined to card interiors. The margin pair are kept apart by 164, which washes every decided state grey in a rail, so the *decided* green never leaves a card interior — a genuine dependency between two decisions rather than a coincidence, and one that breaks the moment a decided state gains a colour in the rail. The two **inside** a card are kept apart only by scale and place: a whole block or a whole button for *decided*, a few words inline for *new wording*. One collision has already been headed off — the editing lane’s focus ring was green and is now blue, because a green box around green words was two meanings an inch apart. Worth a proper look at the palette pass; the likely answer is that *new wording* wants a hue of its own, since it is the only one of the three that is not about a state.

266. **One draft at a time** (raised 2026-08-16; Ed asked what the question was, 2026-08-17). **The question: should starting a second draft somewhere else be possible, and if not, should the surface say so?** There is one caret, so there is one composer: with a draft open, typing elsewhere extends or reuses it rather than starting another. That is almost certainly right — a second unfinished draft is a second thing to lose — and it is what keeps the state model to two flags on an ordinary suggestion. But nobody chose it; it fell out of the implementation. And the surface never says it: pressing ✏️ on a lane while a draft is open quietly extends the open draft instead of warning that you are about to.

267. **Your numbering and this file's numbering collided once** (raised 2026-08-16; Ed asked what this was, 2026-08-17). **The question: nothing that matters — it is a record, and it can be deleted.** On 2026-08-16 you sent three notes numbered 260, 263, 264. The first two matched their questions; the third did not — your "264" was a bug report about the cables, while Q264 in this file is about green meaning several things. I fixed the cables and left Q264 alone, and logged the collision here rather than quietly reconciling it, because the numbering is the thing this file exists to keep honest. Now that you have seen it, it can go.

278. **Signed or anonymous — the surface** (mechanism **settled by Ed 2026-08-17 and folded into SPEC v0.19**; the surface **postponed by Ed 2026-08-17** until the founding-ceremony work, which is where it belongs — the ceremony is what asks these questions) (§3.5a, §9.0a, Appendix A). Kept here only for what is *not* settled: nothing about the surface. Nobody has designed how the founding ceremony asks these questions, how a signed rationale renders beside the blank-disc ones it sits among, or what the record shows when judgments are revealed after a decision. The mechanism half is below.

    **The faces half is closed** (Q854, 2026-08-26). `CLAUDE.md`'s `me` entry had hung *whether other people's faces may appear at all* on this question, and it was never this question's to answer. **Showing who is in the room is membership, and the register is public**: §9.0c settles a name and a picture as personal, binding nobody, and `MemberRowView` in `packages/constitution/src/view.ts` projects name, picture and arrival to every seat by design, with its own note saying why. What §3.5a seals is **whose name is on a proposal** — a document showing fourteen named people and not one named candidate is the ordinary case, and the membership names never pass through the anonymity rung, the list reading the roster row directly. So faces on the register, on the Members list and on the Founded line are not a disclosure question, and no §3.4 citation belongs anywhere near them: §3.4 is *Speech*. What stays open above is the authorship surface and nothing else.

282. **A rendered document and a source offset do not agree** (raised 2026-08-17, from 279's build; Ed asked for a recommendation the same day). The charter fixture is plain text, so the composer can take a caret offset straight from the DOM and use it against the clause's source. The moment the *document* renders markdown that stops being true: a DOM selection measures the rendered text, and `**bold**` is four characters there and eight in the source, so every offset the composer computes is wrong by two for each mark above the caret.

**Recommendation: render markdown in the document, and make the rendered→source mapping a property of the block rather than of the composer.** One function that takes a block element and a DOM position and returns a *source* offset, used by everything that asks the question — `offsetIn`, `startDraftFromTyping`, `startDraftFromRun`. The arithmetic already exists (`richToSource` / `sourceToRich`, written for the lane's view toggle); what matters is that exactly one place knows about the distinction, because the failure mode here is a two-character drift that nothing complains about and no test catches unless the test happens to put a mark above the caret.

Two other options, for the record. Keep the charter plain and let markdown live only inside candidates — cheapest, but an adopted candidate would then print its asterisks into the charter, so it only defers the problem to the first adoption. Or store the document rendered and treat markdown purely as import/export — which gives up "the characters you see are what is stored", and that was the whole reason 279 chose source-of-truth markdown in the first place.

**Do it before the charter carries any formatting**, not after. Until then every test passes and the bug does not exist; the day somebody bolds a word near the top of a long clause it exists everywhere at once, and it will look like a caret bug rather than an offset bug.

**Deferred past the alpha, deliberately** (Ed, 2026-08-27, answering this from the alpha-readiness plan's Track 3.3 — plan-queue **77**, backlog entry 77; the plan had said *fix before the day*). Option **(b)**: the charter stays plain text for the alpha and the rendering is done afterwards. Nothing changes before the day.

Three facts the deferral rests on, checked in the tree at `0d160ad` rather than assumed. **The charter renders plain text today** — `design/session.js:2994` and `:3009` emit `esc(line.x)`, so a clause carrying `**bold**` prints its asterisks — which means the bug is not merely unobserved but *unreachable*: `offsetIn` (`design/session.js:1875`) measures rendered text, and for a plain-text document that **is** the source. **The recommendation above is not a bug fix**, it is *render markdown in the document*, a visible change to what every reader of every charter sees; the arithmetic it needs already exists as `richToSource` / `sourceToRich` (`design/cards.js:342`, `:352`), but the change also moves `design/tools/card-copy.golden.json`, both frozen probes, `scripts/journey-walk.mjs`'s caret and propose steps, and what `design/STYLE.md` takes a clause to be. And **the alpha is short**: reaching the bug needs somebody to bold a word *and* that proposal to win, where the measured preset produces about one adoption per fifteen-minute session (`PRODUCTION.md`'s running log, 2026-08-27).

What the deferral costs, stated so nobody rediscovers it as a surprise: **if an alpha room does adopt a clause carrying a mark, that document's offsets are quietly wrong from then on** — in a throwaway document, which is the whole of why this is affordable. The two other options are on the record above and neither is rejected; (c), building only the block-to-source mapper without changing what renders, remains the cheap half of (a) if the rendering is wanted in a hurry.

288. **A flatter glyph set for the lifecycle marks** (Ed, 2026-08-17, alongside making the gutter marks read as buttons: "I think changing to a flatter emoji pack and making them a bit larger would help"). The larger half is built, along with a resting ground and a press. The pack is a real question, because the design series is **single self-contained files with no network**, so there is no CDN to point at. Three ways: (a) inline the glyphs as hand-authored flat SVGs — total control, they would take the lifecycle hue as a fill, and it is the only option that actually looks designed rather than borrowed; it costs drawing nine symbols and they then have to work at 16px. (b) `font-variant-emoji: text`, which asks the OS for the *text* presentation of the same characters — one line of CSS, genuinely flatter, and it hands the glyph over to `color`; but the shapes are then whatever the platform has, and it would take colour away from the emoji that currently carry their own, which the contents rail depends on (260). (c) Embed an existing flat pack (Twemoji is CC-BY) as data URIs — flat and consistent, but it is somebody else's drawing style and a licence line in a mockup. Recommendation: (a), and only once the alphabet has stopped moving, since it makes every future change to a mark a drawing job.

293. **Revising your own proposal** (Ed, 2026-08-17): *people will often draft something, submit it, then immediately want to edit it again because they saw a mistake — we should think about how to make this action convenient.* Today the path is withdraw, then write again: two acts, and the candidate leaves the race in between. The obvious affordance is a **✏️ on your own proposal card** that reopens the composer seeded from your own wording and, on Propose, replaces the live candidate rather than adding one. It composes out of moves the spec already has (withdraw refunds in full §3.3a, propose costs one) so it is **net zero edits**, which is right — a typo fix should not be priced like a second opinion.

What it needs a ruling on is the evidence. A revision is a **new candidate**: any comparisons already cast against the old wording were cast against a text that no longer exists, so they are discarded exactly as a ground shift discards yours (§4.4). That is correct and it is also a real cost — revise a proposal that six people have already judged and you have thrown their work away, and they will be served the pair again. Three ways. (a) **Always allowed, evidence always resets** — simple, honest, and quietly expensive on a busy race. (b) **A grace window**: free revision until the first judgment lands on it, and after that it is withdraw-and-repropose with the cost visible. This matches what the request is actually about — *immediately*, because they saw a mistake — and it makes the common case free and the expensive case explicit. (c) Revision only as **surgery on the wording**, with evidence carried over where the change is below some edit-distance floor; cheapest for the room and the hardest to explain, since it makes the ranking depend on a diff threshold. Recommendation: **(b)**, with the card saying which side of the window it is on.

296. **The experience of writing a patch** (Ed, 2026-08-17). The cable half is done — a patch is now joined at its cards by a cable in the `chip-gutter`, leaving each `clause-tab` by its left side (see the glossary). What remains: `draft-site` says a second edit somewhere else turns your draft into a patch in the making, and the mockup renders that, but nobody has designed the *experience* — how you know you are now writing one judgment in several places, how you move between the sites while drafting, whether a site can be dropped, and what the single Propose is committing you to. Both are unstarted.

301. **The `deadlock-card`** — built 2026-08-17, and building it found the real question. Settled by Ed: it is its own card, not a race card with a button; it carries **no standings and therefore no lock** (303 — a card showing only wordings and rationales leaks nothing, since both are already public, so it takes nothing away and needs no rule); and it does not "replace" a race card, because by the time you see it the race has nothing left to ask you (304 dissolved). Built on the § Sanctions fixture with **eight** wordings at Ed's request.

What eight showed: the field is a wall of near-identical sentences. Two attempts at fixing it are recorded as dead ends. **Marking against the clause** (`result-only`, what every other card does) marks only three of the eight — the floor in `wordingHtml` is measured against the incumbent, and a wording this far from the clause is a rewrite rather than an edit, so it states itself plain by design (Q92) — and half a field wearing green reads as arbitrary. **Ranking** is unavailable by construction: being level is what deadlock means. The finding underneath both: **at eight, the comparison you need is not each-against-the-clause but each-against-the-others**, and nothing computed against the incumbent can draw that. 305 and 306 are the two ways out.

Marking the field green (Ed, 2026-08-17) took most of the weight off both of these: with the floor forced off, the shared spine stays black and each attempt 's own move lights up, and eight wordings are scannable in one pass. 305 and 306 are improvements now rather than rescues, and neither should be built without Ed reading the marked card first.

305. **Per-candidate advisory labels** (raised 2026-08-17). A six-word label per wording saying what *this* one does differently — *removal only after a broken sanction* · *neutral proposer* · *removal is not a sanction* · *both, plus a night's sleep* · *twice in a year* · *sanctions expire* · *two-thirds for removal* · *ask the visitor first*. It makes a field of eight scannable in one pass, and you read the full wording only for the ones you care about. Same family as `race-labeler` and the same discipline — advisory, outside the state machine, never in the event log, never gating anything — but it is a **new** model output: the labeler names *races*, and this names *candidates*. It is also the cheapest possible version of what a camp map does, since two candidates with near-identical labels are visibly the same camp without anything having to say so. Recommended first, because it is useful on any card with more than two wordings, not only a deadlocked one.

306. **The `camp-map`, finally built** (raised 2026-08-17). Group the field by camp, each under a heading saying what that camp will not give up — *removal must not be reachable in one step* / *the ladder is fine, the proposer must be neutral*. For the job the deadlock card exists for this is the most useful thing that could be on it: a bridge has to satisfy both, and until you can name them you are guessing. Two costs. It is **judgment-derived** — which wordings cluster is computed from how people judged — so unlike 305 it is not free of SPEC §3.5, which lumps camps in with standings as briefing-only. The defensible refinement is that **structure without direction is a magnitude**, which §8.3 already permits: saying *the room divides in two, and here is what each half wants* names no leader and no sizes. That is a real spec amendment and wants Ed rather than me. Second cost: it is the one thing here that could be wrong in a way the reader cannot check.

308. **The one overhang a fitted pile cannot remove** (raised 2026-08-17). `fitStacks()` shrinks a stack's peek until it reaches no further than the next mark in the gutter, which makes two anchors claiming one strip structurally impossible — measured across all 32 columns in the fixture, zero collisions. But a pile's floor is **one tab**, 30px, and there is nothing to shrink when a clause holds only one. A block shorter than 30px whose next mark is within 30px would still overlap, and headings are 17–19px. It cannot happen in this fixture and it is not hypothetical: `always-on-typing` covers headings, so a heading can carry a decision. Two ways. (a) A **layout pass that pushes down** — no mark may start above the previous one's bottom, so a mark steps out of the way rather than overlapping, which is precisely what the `needs-you-queue` already does around a pinned card. Costs the rule that a mark stands beside its own text, in exactly the cases where it cannot. (b) **Give short blocks a floor** in the gutter — a minimum block height wherever a mark sits. Deforms the charter to suit the margin, which is the wrong way round. Recommendation: **(a)**, and only when a case actually appears, since it is invisible today.

310. **The three per-type notes on a decision card** (raised 2026-08-17, housekeeping pass). A race card, a patch card and a salience diagonal each carry one grey sentence saying what that kind of card does: *neither of these has to win — the clause above stands unless the leader clears the bar*; *one judgment for all 3 places — choosing here chooses everywhere*; *this ranks the questions, never the answers*. They are the same species as the three footnotes Ed cut on 2026-08-17 — the `yoursnote`, the revise line, the deadlock caveat — by the test he was applying: a sentence that appears on every card of its kind is a design note rather than information. Two things make these different, which is why they are a question rather than a deletion. They are per **type** rather than per card, so a member meets each of them a handful of times and not on every judgment. And each says something the card cannot say any other way: a race has no radio that keeps the clause, a patch's single ✓ commits in three places you cannot all see, and a diagonal is the one card that changes no text. Three ways. (a) **Keep them**, and treat "one line per card *type*" as a device the surface owns. (b) **Cut them**, and let the type declare itself — a race by having two candidates and no keep-radio, a patch by its ↑/↓ stepper and place count, a diagonal by its head being a question. (c) **Move them into the field label**, which already names the type (*Proposed · 2 rival proposals*), so the fact and its consequence are in one place. Recommendation: **(c)** for the race, where the label already says *rival*; (a) for the patch, whose note warns of a consequence rather than naming a shape. They were moved **above** the commit row in the same pass regardless of this, because the commit row is the card's bottom band everywhere else.

311. **Is a locked judgment card without a ground shift reachable?** (raised 2026-08-17, housekeeping pass). `reviseNote` has a branch for `s.locked` that says *this one is settled, so your judgment is on the record as it stands (SPEC §4.4)*, and `commitRowHtml` drops the ✓ for it. No fixture reaches it: the only locked card in the mockup is also ground-shifted, and `shifted` is tested first. So a whole card state exists in the code and has never been drawn. The doubt is whether it can exist at all — a race that settles becomes a **sealed record**, which is a different card, so it is not obvious what leaves a *judgment* card locked. Either it is a real intermediate state (settled but not yet sealed, or sealed while you had it open) and wants a fixture so it can be looked at, or it is not and the branch and the `locked` flag should go with it.

312. **A ↻ never has its re-served question beside it** (raised 2026-08-17, answering Ed's question about the ↻ lifecycle). SPEC §4.4 says a ground shift locks your old judgment *and* serves the pairs on the new ground fresh, and §6.2 gives a re-opened race a 1.5× routing boost so it comes back **sooner** than an ordinary question. So in the mechanism a ↻ is a receipt standing next to a live question — the locked fact about text that no longer exists, and the same race asking again. In the mockup it is a **terminus**: § The Garden carries exactly one entry, the ↻ itself, and there is no fresh 💡 anywhere near it. `shifted` is a fixture flag that nothing sets or clears at runtime, so the surface renders the state and never the transition into or out of it. Two consequences worth a ruling. **The pairing has never been looked at** — a member meeting a ground shift for the first time meets it as *your judgment is void* with nothing offered in exchange, when the actual experience should be *that one is void, here is the question again*, and whether those are two rail entries at one clause or one entry that carries both is a real design question nobody has asked. **And the mark may be wrong on its own terms**: ↻ was chosen to mean *comes back around, rebuilt* (glossary), which is a promise the surface currently never keeps. Cheap to fix in the fixture — a second entry at § The Garden, judged-fresh, would show the pair — and that is probably the first step, because it is hard to design the pairing without seeing it.

313. **Nothing acknowledges the work** (found 2026-08-17 running Ed's flame-to-flame workflow end to end: click 🔥, judge, click the next 🔥, repeat until the queue is empty). The mechanics are sound — eighteen judgments, every flame opening on the first click, the flame handing over every time, the clause never moving, the salience diagonal arriving when the queue emptied — and the whole run is **silent**. Two halves. **It ends with nothing said.** You answer the last question, the diagonal is served, you answer that, and the screen is simply a screen with nothing on it that wants you. Q291 recorded a third state — a panel saying the priorities are as clear as more answers would make them — and it went out with the *offer* when Ed changed the diagonal to *served, not offered*. That was right about the offer and took the completion state with it by accident: the two are different things, and one of them is the only moment the surface has to say *you are done here*. **And nothing accumulates.** The topbar's *confidence bar 74%* and *quorum 5 of 14* are static markup, never re-rendered; eighteen judgments move neither. In the mockup that is a fidelity gap rather than a bug, but it is the place a session's vital signs live, and a workflow made entirely of judging is precisely the one that never touches them — so the member with the highest contribution is the one shown the least evidence that contributing did anything. Worth deciding together, because the answer might be one thing: if the bar visibly moved as judgments landed, the end of the queue might need no panel at all. Related: the `room-pulse` already beats for your own actions, so the surface has a place that says *something happened* and no place that says *something changed*.

314. **Seeing which card other members have open** (Ed, 2026-08-17, and **shelved by him in the same breath** — *let's shelve this for now until we actually test with a group of real humans, otherwise this is quite speculative*). The idea: the surface shows who is judging what, right now. Recorded rather than acted on, with the two things that make it worth returning to and the one that makes it hard. It is the natural extension of the `room-pulse`, which already says *the room is moving* and deliberately says nothing else; presence is the next rung and the first one with content in it. And it is the only proposal so far that would tell you a live *question* is being worked on rather than that the document is. What makes it genuinely hard is that it collides with SPEC §3.5: knowing that eleven people are looking at one race is a fact about where the attention is, which is close to a standing, and knowing *who* is on a card is a step towards knowing how they judged it in a room of fourteen. Ed's instinct to wait for real humans is the right test — this is exactly the kind of thing that reads as either warm or surveilling depending on the room, and no amount of reasoning here settles which.

319. **Where do the setup piles live once drafting starts?** (raised 2026-08-18, the loose end in Ed's *when the ceremony is done we end up with a tab-group at the top of the document next to the title*.) Both setup surfaces now end in exactly that, and `session-view` has nothing of the kind: a live member cannot ask what the bar is, who may be seen, or when the window closes without leaving the document. The obvious answer is that they simply **stay** — it is at the head of the charter, every tab grey, every card read-only, and clicking one opens it over the top of the document exactly as it did during setup. Which would make setup and session literally the same screen in the last place they still differ. Two things to settle first: whether two permanent piles at the top of a charter earn their space in a session where nobody is asking about them, and what happens on a **perpetual** document, where §9.5's freeze and thaw mean the constitution can be re-opened and the group would have live tabs again.


325. *(Partly overtaken 2026-08-18 evening: the file merged into `design/setup.html` (361), so no file carries the name any more — what remains to decide is only what the glossary calls the stage.)* **Should `founding-ceremony` keep its name now that “ceremony” has left the surface?** (raised 2026-08-18.) Nothing a member reads says *ceremony* any more — the page is *Before we begin*, the topbar says *before we begin*, and the rule is *everyone answers, and when everyone is ready the document begins*. But the file, the glossary entry and half the reasoning still call it the founding ceremony, which is now a private word for a thing the product calls something else, and that is exactly the drift the glossary exists to stop. Three ways. (a) **Rename to `opening-questions`** — literal, matches the copy, and `design/opening-questions.html` says what it is. (b) **Keep `founding-ceremony` as the internal name** on the grounds that it names the *stage* precisely and internal names may be more technical than copy — which is true of `race` and `adoption-threshold` already. (c) **Rename the concept but keep the file**, which is the worst of both. Recommendation: **(a)**, done as its own mechanical pass so the diff is a rename and nothing else.

326. **“Participant” in the engine** (raised 2026-08-18, alongside *I’d like to change “participants” to “members”*). The surfaces are swept, but `participant-api`, `scripted-persona`/`llm-persona`’s docs and a good deal of SPEC still say participant — and there it is arguably the right word, because the whole point of that module is that a human, a sim persona and a machine member are **interchangeable behind one interface**, which is a claim about participants and not about membership. Two ways. (a) **Leave the engine alone** and treat “participant” as strictly internal, never appearing in copy — which is what §9.8 now says. (b) **Rename throughout** for one vocabulary, at the cost of a wide mechanical diff across engine-core, sim-harness and SPEC, and of losing the word that says *whatever speaks this API*. Recommendation: **(a)**, and it is already written; this is here so the decision is on the record rather than implied.

331. **Should a settled setting be titled differently from an outstanding one?** (Ed answered the first half on 2026-08-18: *whatever seems easiest to understand — can you tell what the type of the answer is by the title?* So a title says what kind of answer it wants, and consistency is not the point; four titles changed and two terms of art stayed nouns.) What is left is the half his rule does not reach. A card outstanding is a **question**, and *Is there a machine member?* is exactly right for it. The same card settled is a **rule**, and it stays in the pile at the head of the document as part of the constitution — where *Approval threshold, 66%* reads better than *How sure must the room be? 66%*, because a constitution is a list of what is so and not a list of what was asked. Three ways. (a) **One title throughout**, the question, and accept that the head of the document reads as an interview. (b) **Two labels on one card**, the question while it wants you and the noun once it is settled — truer to both, at the cost of a rule about which shows and of a tab whose name changes under you. (c) **The question is the title and the noun is the value line**, which is nearly what happens already: *When does it end?* over *Thursday 18:00*. Recommendation: **(c)**, on the grounds that it needs nothing new — the pile is read by its glyphs and its values far more than by its titles, and a card nobody has to re-learn is worth more than a heading that reads perfectly.

335. **Split `Constitution` into what the room agreed and what the engine is tuned to** (raised 2026-08-18, PLAN.md §2.2; expanded after Ed asked why it matters). engine-core has one object holding both — the threshold, the grant, the drip, the stake and the window on one side, and `hotSetSize`, `explorationEvery`, `boutGapMs`, `deadlockEpsilon`, `cooldownMs`, `redraftLimit` on the other. Three things go wrong while they share a name. The **record** published at the close would list a tuning constant beside the threshold as though the room had agreed it. The **motion** machinery routes on whether a setting is constitutional, and with one object there is nothing stopping somebody moving to amend `boutGapMs`. And the **ceremony** has no definable output: what the founding questions produce *is* the room’s half of this object, and it cannot produce half of a thing. The cost is a rename across engine-core and sim-harness today, against a data migration once real documents exist with saved settings. Recommendation: **now**. The only reason it is a question at all is that it touches a package that should not change without Ed’s word. **Status 2026-08-18:** the in-depth review Ed asked for is delivered as REVIEW-creation-session.md; its §7 is the definitive field-by-field mapping, and the split is one commit once he has read it (338’s answer decides one field’s home). *The document-level half landed 2026-08-22 (spec pass 1, Q562): Appendix A is now* Engine tuning *and holds only what nobody is asked; the settings are §9.7.1. The engine-core object is still one.*

346. **Sybil resistance at the front door** (Ed, 2026-08-18, parked: *if people can become members via a link, we have to be wary of sybil attacks — perhaps even accidental*). *Note, 2026-08-26 (entry 94): the open door is now 🤝 *yes* with 🪪 at ✒️ — an application admitted on submit — which is exactly where this bites; the same price at 🏛️ or ✏️ puts a room between the link and the membership.* Membership begins at first arrival (§9.6a) and the magic link **is** the credential, so the attack surface is the invitation itself. Ed’s own example is the subtlest and the most likely: **one person accidentally becoming two members on two devices** — possible only if membership is minted per *arrival* rather than per *invitation*, so the structural rule is that however many devices open a link, they land in one membership: a link must never create an identity, only unlock one. Three more vectors, one of them innocent: a **forwarded link** logs the bearer in *as the invitee* — two people quietly sharing one membership, or a colleague “helpfully” opening a link that was not theirs; a **double invitation** (one person at two addresses) makes one person two members, two wallets, two ceremony answers and two places in quorum; and deliberate padding by whoever controls invitations. Sharpened by §9.6a itself: a duplicate discovered **after the start** cannot be removed, because removal is a constitutional motion needing every member’s consent including the duplicate’s — so whatever the defence is, it has to work **before** judging opens. Candidate defences for later, cheapest first: (a) first-open **confirmation** (“you are Ida Fenwick — is that you?”), with later opens of the same link joining the same membership rather than a new one — note the tension with single-use links, which would lock the person’s second device out instead; (b) the convenor’s pre-start window is the human check — arrivals are visible, uninviting is free, and the start is the deadline for noticing; (c) a **merge** operation for discovered duplicates that is not an expulsion (one person’s two memberships becoming one re-rates nothing they did not do themselves) — needs care where both halves answered the ceremony differently. Deliberate sybil by an adversarial *convenor* is out of scope: they control the roster before the start anyway, and the defence against a bad-faith convenor is not joining their document.

349. **Crown residuals** (raised 2026-08-18, around §9.7’s 👑). Ed settled the shape — assent ends either route on any reserved setting, lapse is automatic assent, the 👑 follows any reservation, and (v0.52) the founder may delegate anything once proposing opens, which closed the old (b). Two edges remain. (a) **Whose clock?** A crowned convenor may be a clerk, who casts no judgments — does administering (inviting, changing ordinary settings) count as the activity that keeps a crown alive, or only member-acts? Recommendation: any authenticated act on the document resets the clock; the crown lapses on absence, not on abstinence from drafting. (c) **Does the crown cover removal during the ceremony’s blind collection?** A crowned convenor removing an answered member mid-ceremony is §9.6a’s ground-shift case already; nothing new needed — note and close.

350. **Setup-grammar residuals** (raised 2026-08-18, the pass that put setup tasks on the session-view lifecycle — Ed set the frame: *yellow when open, ⏳ while waiting for other input, grey when decided, they lose their custom emoji and just become ✔s*; these are the calls made inside it, each cheap to flip). (a) **⏳ entries stay in the rail**, their fill the watching UI — pile-only was tried on paper and fails concretely: mid-verification the rail would be empty with the mail modal closed and no handle left to reopen it. Gates still wait as pile tabs only, per their own rule. (b) **No 🔥 on setup**: the dependency chain already serves the founding one question at a time, and every setup question is mandatory — a flame chooses among optional judgments, which is a category this surface does not have. (c) **Owed decisions are news-green**: a member's un-acknowledged constitutional settings, a resolved blind question (for everyone who answered it, convenor included), and an opening gate all wear the changed wash with a drawn ✔ and pin until OK — superseding gate-cards' earlier *yellow with an OK*, because on the live surface an unacknowledged decision is green news, not a hot ask. (d) ~~All-✔ piles~~ closed by Ed same day: **a retired tab keeps its subject glyph** — the piles are a menu — so only the rail entry retires to ✔; the constitution block's lines stay buttons besides. (e) **The applicant wizard keeps its done tasks visible** as a checklist — an admitted departure from done-leaves-the-rail, defended by the four tasks being that surface's whole content.

354. **The closed-document surface** (raised 2026-08-18, from Ed's lifecycle check before the cards.js extraction: *I expect just the document where you can no longer take actions* — confirmed with two refinements, residuals here). The frame that fell out for free: **grey means nothing is asked of you, and a closed document is a document that is entirely grey** — same three columns, empty rail, every clause-tab a filed pile still openable to its sealed record, the constitution at the head. Residuals: (a) how `record-builder`'s publication (rankings, camps, graveyard, minority map, audit log) renders *on-surface* — a Record section at the foot, or reachable only through each clause's own filed pile; (b) the 👤/✍️/👁️ reveal settings mostly resolve "at the close" — does closing re-render the sealed speakers as named people in place, and judgments likewise; (c) **frozen vs closed presentation** — a freeze is a stall with an alarm, not an ending, so the frozen surface must look *waiting* (parked races, the thaw path visible) where the closed one looks *finished*; nothing yet distinguishes them visually. The extraction makes this surface a third consumer of the shared card grammar rather than a new build.

367. **Engine catch-up — (a) AND (b) are BUILT.** (a) 2026-08-18, plan 367a: `@draft/constitution`, tested, simulated, bundled, `setup.html` rendering over it. (b) 2026-08-19: the four race-adjacent deltas landed in engine-core — quorum in §4.2's floor (F = max(Q, min(⌈E/3⌉, F_max)), share-quorums tracking E), the real-minutes drip (Q353; `tokenDripMinutes`, perpetual documents drip, the adapter's lossy quarantine dissolved), amendments binding races in flight (§9.6/Q328: `amend` + `standing-set` events, §4.3 threshold anchors — a bar never jumps, drip re-phase without retro-credit), participant suspension (§9.5a's lapse engine-side), and **setting races** (Q390, SPEC v0.53: ordinary motions ride the race machinery whole — standing value as incumbent, rival values in one race, equality dedup, verdict-not-application adoption). The engine went **node-free** (pure sha256, parity-tested), and `packages/constitution/src/engine-bridge.ts` marries the two sessions: motions race in the engine and adjudicate through the same `adjudicateOrdinaryMotion` seam the mock's dev button spoke. Evidence: `npm run motions -w @draft/sim-harness` (narrative walks: the raced motion, the crown's verdict-then-assent-then-ground, an amendment ground-shifting a race in flight). **Residuals**, each deliberate: the mock still adjudicates by its dev seam — wiring the browser engine in is Q391's design work; §8.3a's diagonal gate (volume/audience/2E-stream, replacing `salienceEvery`) is still unimplemented engine-side and `salienceEvery` still exists pending the Q335 split; admit-motions (§9.7½) have no scalar value to race and stay on the seam (NOTES.md); and engine bar-amendment anchors glide where the cs display re-seeds (NOTES.md — the engine is the adjudicating authority). Next per Ed's ordering: **Q368**, the thin server.

368. **The thin product server — BUILT** (Ed, 2026-08-19: *368 let's go!*; landed the same day as `packages/server`, `@draft/server`). node:http, no framework, ~700 lines: documents persist as their own hash-chained JSONL logs (loading is `replay`, chain verified — no database, no second truth), creation and login are single-use mailed magic links (§9.7a: nothing exists until the creation link is followed; §9.6a: following an invitation arrives you), sessions are stateless HMAC cookies, every command runs as the cookie's member through a whitelist (`commands.ts`) and `view()` is the only read served, mail rides the fold (invitations, lapse warnings, the lapse package — Resend with `RESEND_API_KEY`, the `data/outbox.jsonl` dev inbox without), `tick()` drives the clocks a minute apart, and every slug a document has worn routes to it. `npm run server` at the root; end-to-end test walks create → invite → arrive → constitute → motion over a real socket and replays the disk log to an identical rolling hash. **Residuals** (NOTES.md): the applicant flow (module ready, token dance unbuilt); the engine-bridge is not attached (ordinary motions record but nothing races server-side — where the engine session lives is Q391's design); the page still runs its fixture (wiring setup.html to this API is Q391); rate limiting is Q346 territory; deployment is Ed's call and nothing deploys itself.

384. **A creation-time holder choice for the title and link?** (raised 2026-08-19, unanswered in chat.) v0.52 has them *begin* reserved, with delegation available from the moment proposing opens — read off Ed's "the option to unreserve either or both as soon as proposals open". A radio at creation would let a founder start them delegated; probably noise (there is nobody to hold them for until the room exists), but cheap if wanted.

386. **No surface yet raises a reserve motion** (raised 2026-08-19, unanswered in chat; generalized by v0.52). The module carries the `reserve` payload on any held-able setting and the page renders such motions defensively, but no composer offers "return this to the founder's reserve" — it wants a home, probably a second option on a delegated setting's own composer, in the same sentence grammar the delegation itself now uses.

387. **Reserving the text** (Ed, 2026-08-19, kept out of scope *for now*): every change to the document needing the founder's assent to merge — against the spirit of the product, and also exactly how suggested amendments work in many text-editing products, which is why it is parked rather than dead. If it ever lands, it is a fourth thing the 👑 can hold, and the assent step already exists (§9.7).

388. **Individual delegation, foundership transfer, inheritance** (Ed, 2026-08-19, out of scope — *maybe when we make the sequel!*): founders delegating powers to individual members rather than the room; transferring the foundership deliberately or on lapse. The 👑 is a benevolent dictator for life, not a literal monarch — nobody inherits it when the founder lapses (v0.49's auto-assent is the whole story of an absent crown).

389. **Closed 2026-08-26 — built as the doors (entry 94, SPEC v0.74 §9.7 rule 9):** the register's crown became its own thing, ✉️ and ❌ each holding a ✒️/🛡️ pair over the act; 🤝's pair is the policy's alone. **The applications card's two holds read as one** (raised 2026-08-19, from the v0.52 build). The 🤝 card holds the membership's crown (in its value) and the policy setting itself (its holder); v0.52 delegates both with one sentence — "Applications are delegated to the members" — on the reading that a half-delegated door is not a state anybody wants. If a founder should be able to hand over the join policy while keeping invite-and-remove (or vice versa), the card needs two sentences, and the register's crown probably becomes its own setting rather than a field of this one. *v0.54 makes this concrete: the register's holder now carries the two powers (unilateral, assent) inside the applications value — Ed's own example splits them — so the card has real states to show either way.*

391. **The live page — BUILT** (Ed, 2026-08-19: *4 and 5 let's go!*; landed the same day). setup.html speaks the server: at `/` it is the §9.7a birth — the email step mints the real creation mail, pasted text rides the pending stash (v0.55), and in dev the mail preview's button follows the actual magic link; at `/d/:slug` it renders the real document through a ConstitutionSession-lookalike over `view()` (`remoteCS`) — blind on the wire, every command a serialized POST folded as the cookie's member, values re-hydrating on a 4s poll but never under the card the viewer has open, dev chrome hidden, arrival never a page act (§9.6a). Ordinary motions **race in the engine at the server** (`engine-host`: the bridge is born the moment the constitution settles, persisted as engine.jsonl + bridge.json beside the document log, resumed by replay), and the page's ordinary judgment commits as a real pairwise `judge-race` on the served card — the dev majority seam never runs against a live document. Walked end to end in a browser: birth → magic link → invitations → arrival links → blind founding → constituted → a member's rate motion raced, judged at the adoption floor, parked behind the 👑, accepted on the page, applied, stake refunded with performance — and bit-identical after a server restart. **Residuals, each deliberate**: (a) rival values on one setting still render as the first motion's card — the multi-candidate race card is session-view design work wanting Ed's QA; (b) a running ordinary motion's judged tally shows only the viewer's own judgment (the blind view serves no counts for races — §3.5's discipline until motion disclosure is thought through); (c) the slug is server-assigned pre-save from the title (changeable after; no link ever breaks); (d) reading a `public`/`link` chamber without a membership cookie is unbuilt — every view currently requires login; (e) the applicant's own four-card seat is still fixture — but the whole apply→verify→submit→race→admit road runs live since Q397 (v0.56), the members judging each applicant from the Applicants block on the 🪪 card; (f) 4s polling, not push; (g) — **fixed 2026-08-19** (Ed's QA: *I can't seem to suggest removing a member*): the register counts as settled for the composer gate, so the 🪪 card composes invitations and removals on both surfaces; the founder's direct-invite body survives only where the direct power is real.

393. **§8.3a diagonal gate — BUILT** (Ed, 2026-08-19: *build it*; landed the same day). The feed serves no diagonal below E live questions (a race counts once, deadlocked included); from E, only to a participant with nothing else to judge (an unjudged deadlocked race counts as work, §8.3b), by active pair selection over the salience fit, terminating at the races' own deadlockEpsilon and at three in a row; from 2E the ~1-in-`salienceEvery` stream returns for everybody. `salienceEvery` survives as that stream rate — §8.3a's third regime uses "the old ~1 in 10", so Q335's delete-the-knob note is half-superseded: the knob keeps a job. Tests: engine-core test/diagonal-gate.test.ts.

401. **(b only — (a) closed by Ed 2026-08-19, *perfect*, and built: SPEC v0.60; the founder-direct removal path closed 2026-08-26, built as ❌'s ✒️, exile at will and immediate — entry 94, v0.74.)** Under the 🥾 removal setting's **proposal** rung (*ordinary* until v0.74), may the subject judge their own removal race? Today nothing stops them: the module puts no per-race judge restriction, the mock counts every member's judgment, and the engine race (`remove:<id>`) serves them pairs like anybody. Arguments each way: their §3.3-style stake in the outcome is exactly the author's-own-preference case the spec already counts; against, a removal race with the subject judging is the one race whose electorate the constitutional rungs deliberately shape, and the ordinary rung silently un-shapes it.

406. **Subsection headings don't fold** (raised 2026-08-19, from the heading-click build). Clicking a heading's text folds it — prose headings, the constitution's sections, and the Constitution heading itself — but the lvl3 subsection headings ("Members" in Membership) carry no fold state: the membership body is hand-composed and the fold machinery is keyed per section. Worth folding too, or is one level of folding inside the constitution enough?

417. **The setup surface has no wallet, so nothing teaches what a proposal costs** (raised 2026-08-19, from the copy pass). Ed removed "Costs one ✏️, which you get back if you withdraw it" on the right grounds — *the flying pencil teaches this* — but the flying pencil is session-view's, and setup.html has neither an `edit-wallet` in its topbar nor either flight. So a member composing a motion there now sees no price at all, and the withdraw hands back something they never saw leave. Three things would have to travel together, and only the first is mechanical: (a) the wallet in the topbar, its drip and its cap; (b) `arcFrames` / `refundFlight` / the `walletShow`+`walletGhost` render-state pair, lifted out of session-view into the shared layer — which means cards.js, and therefore a session-probe run against the frozen reference; (c) **the gesture**, which is a real design choice rather than a port: session-view spends an edit on a *hold* (the pencil's flight is the progress meter), while a motion here commits on a *click*, and a click has no duration for a pencil to cross. Either the ordinary motion commit becomes a hold like session-view's, or the outbound flight becomes something a click can carry (the pencil leaving the wallet as the card closes). The constitutional route is unaffected — 🏛️ is already a ten-second hold and costs no ✏️.

398. **The multi-candidate ordinary race card** (raised 2026-08-19, from the Q391 build; Q390 made rivals real). Rival values on one setting join one engine race, but the page still renders “the first running motion” as a two-way card (what stands vs the proposal). The engine serves rival pairs with the conditional prompt (“if this text changes, which change is better?”, Q48) and the served card is already in the view — what is missing is session-view card grammar for a setting race with three or more lanes, and the rule for which motion card a second rival renders into. Wants Ed's design pass, not a mechanical fix.

419. **Does the desktop focus order match the reading order?** (mobile plan, 2026-08-20). The phone fix for DOM order is `order:` on grid children precisely so the DOM stays canonical — but nobody has ever tabbed through the desktop surface, and if that order is wrong the phone inherits it. *Answered by Ed 2026-08-23 as recommended in `design/MOBILE.md` §4 (2026-08-23): no `order:` anywhere; the desktop pass tabs the page and asserts DOM order.*
